Every sheet of a spreadsheet that holds a table is read: a file with several such sheets has its columns under source_file = "file.xlsx [sheet]", and the sheet column names the sheet of any spreadsheet with several sheets; a sheet of notes or a codebook is treated as documentation when the file has another table. Following DDI, a column is described by orthogonal facets rather than a single type: representation (numeric/text/datetime/code — how it is stored), measurement_level (Stevens: nominal/ordinal/interval/ratio), concept (what it measures: reaction_time/age/gender/likert/id/…), role (identifier/measure/condition/timestamp), unit, and a quality state (ok/empty/constant). Concepts are detected by name+value rules (the demographic concepts via data_check_demographic()); when llm_use(TRUE) the model fills concepts and measurement levels the rules left blank. Qualtrics survey exports are recognised (data_check_is_qualtrics()): their extra header rows are stripped so columns type correctly, and the reserved response-metadata columns (StartDate, Duration, Finished, …) are tagged.

The module defaults to rules-only when llm_use(FALSE): columns the rules cannot resolve (ambiguous 3–20-unique integers, ambiguous character columns) fall back to continuous / text respectively. When llm_use(TRUE), ambiguous columns and unresolved file types (other) are sent to llm() for optional refinement, and every file is assigned a study group (ex1, pilot2, shared, …) so that multi-study repositories can be recognised (used by psychds_check). Without an LLM the study group is left NA (unknown).

Column extraction requires files to be readable from disk. Files fetched by repo_check from OSF/GitHub/etc. without a local copy (file_location is NA) are classified but not column-extracted; pass local_path to point at a downloaded copy of the repository.

For a paper whose repository is on Dryad, this means column extraction never happens unless a Dryad API key is set: Dryad requires one to download file bytes, even from a fully public dataset (dataset listing and metadata do not need one). Without a key, every Dryad file stays classified but un-downloaded, column_n is 0, and a warning naming Dryad and ?dryad_pat/?dryad_auth is included among the download failures. A one-time session message is also shown (see repo_check) the first time a Dryad dataset is encountered with no key set.

Every downloaded text file the authors wrote (code, settings, survey definitions such as Qualtrics .qsf files, documentation, outputs) is searched for keys: API keys, access tokens and private keys, which let anyone who downloads the file use a paid service at the authors’ cost. Of a tabular data file only the column names and first 3 rows of the table as read are searched: its values come from participants, but a key the authors set for a whole survey (as embedded data in a Qualtrics survey flow) is exported in every response. The search uses the rules of the secret scanner betterleaks (https://github.com/betterleaks/betterleaks, MIT licence) of high and medium confidence. A key found turns the traffic light red; the report and the keys table give its file, line and type, and only its beginning. Other files over 25 MB and binary files are not searched. A key is never tested with its provider. Every module’s result has any whole key replaced by “keyremoved” (see module_run()).

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Levi Baruch

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the data_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Reproducibility Check works

This module assesses whether a paper’s R and Python code can be run on its data. It works in two phases. The static phase (always run) collects the package dependencies the code declares, rewrites the data-file paths that do not work in the repository as the authors organised it, works out the order the scripts must run in, and diagnoses why any referenced input file is unavailable — reporting what a reproduction attempt would involve and where it is likely to break. The execution phase (opt-in, execute = TRUE) then actually runs the code: it copies the repository into a throwaway directory, optionally installs the declared dependencies into a throwaway library, runs each script in order in an isolated subprocess with a per-script timeout, and captures the outcome and full output of each run.

By default (execute = FALSE) the module does not run any code — it performs only static analysis: reading dependencies with the same extractors as code_check, finding the files the code references in the repository, and building a run-order dependency graph. It reports what would be installed and run rather than doing it.

When execute = TRUE, the module additionally runs the downloaded code on your machine — a deliberate, opt-in action gated behind that argument (callr is required for R code). Each script runs in an isolated subprocess so a crash cannot take down the R session; note this isolates crashes, not the filesystem or network. Installs (when install_missing = TRUE) and the throwaway run library never touch your main R library. Each script’s outcome is one of ran_ok, errored, timed_out, dependency_unavailable, not_parsed, run_by_source (an R script another script runs with source(), which is run only inside that script, since on its own it lacks the packages and objects the sourcing script sets up first), run_by_import (a Python file other files import, a module rather than a script, unless it has an if __name__ == "__main__": block), jags_model (a JAGS or BUGS model saved with an R extension, which another script reads) or not_r_content (a file that is not R at all, such as a JSON or HTML dump); these last two are not run and are not failures. python2 marks Python 2 code that was not run. With sandbox = "docker" such code runs with Python 2.7 (option metacheck.docker_image_py2; FALSE turns this off), which modifications records; it is never converted, since that can change results. defines_functions is a file that only defines functions, which is used, not run (table$used_by names the scripts that use it), and not_analysis is a file of software rather than of the analysis: a package’s code, tests, documentation or setup.py, a copy of a tool, a web application, a script a program wrote (table$not_analysis_why says which); neither is run or counted as a script. docker_failed means the container never started, which says nothing about the script, so it keeps the light from being green but does not make it red. needs_a_person is a script that stopped because it asks a person to choose a file or answer a question, or that starts an app, and needs_internet one that stopped because the network is off in a Docker run, or, with internet = TRUE, could not reach what it asked for; download_too_large is a script stopped for downloading more than the limits allow (see internet), and out_of_memory one that, or a program it started (such as a compiler), ran out of the memory each script may use. All four are treated the same way. not_downloaded is a script that stopped on a file the repository lists but that was not downloaded, such as one over max_file_size: it could not be tried, so it is not counted as a script that did not reproduce (summary_table$repro_not_downloaded_n counts them apart). not_read is a file metacheck could not read, so it was not run; the light is then not green, as the file may read and run on another computer, and table$error_message says why, naming a package that is installed but cannot be loaded on the computer that ran the check. empty is a file with nothing in it, which is not run or counted as a script. For a script that did not run through, table$error_message is its error, short (at most 300 characters): the error line of its output, which names a package that is not there, with the address it asked for when it needed the internet, or the time limit it reached; table$reason sorts it into a kind. A timeout means “still running at the cutoff”, not a failure, so raise timeout for legitimately long-running (e.g. Bayesian) scripts. dependency_unavailable means the script’s own package could not be installed from live CRAN, the CRAN Archive, or a named GitHub/URL source — an infrastructure limitation, not a defect in the paper’s code, so unlike errored/timed_out it does not force the traffic light red. The full stdout/stderr of every run is kept in the result’s run_results (the report shows it in per-script dropdowns, capped for readability). The traffic light reflects the STATIC readiness signals by default, but an execution error (errored/timed_out) does recolour it to red — a script that crashes on its own analysis is a genuine reproduction failure. A script that fails with could not find function is run once more with the library() calls the project’s other scripts make added at its top (or the one package that exports the function, or magrittr for %>%), and a script that then runs is reported as reproducible only after that repair.

Dependencies are reported name-only, with the source (CRAN, GitHub, or a base package already shipped with R) — static analysis of the code cannot know which version was used, and running against current package versions is itself informative: a break argues the authors should have pinned versions. When the repository does record versions, a run with recorded_versions = TRUE installs the packages the scripts load at those versions, but it does not change the R version, which remains a limitation of the check.

Run order is derived from read-after-write data dependencies (a script writing a file another reads must run first), source() edges, and numeric filename prefixes as a weak tie-breaker. Ambiguous orderings, dependency cycles, and inputs a script reads but that no earlier script produces (and that are not in the repository) are surfaced as warnings, since these are the situations most likely to make an otherwise-correct reproduction fail.

Python code (.py files, and notebooks and Quarto documents whose code is Python, from which the code is extracted first) goes through the same two phases, reported in its own “Python code” section. Each file is checked for syntax errors with Python’s own parser when a Python interpreter (python, or else python3) is available; dependencies are read from the import statements, with versions from a requirements.txt or pyproject.toml when there is one, and installed with pip; each script runs as its own python process (processx is required), so unlike R scripts, a name one script defines is not available to another. When no Python interpreter is available, the Python files are reported as not run and the R results are still returned. Statistics the Python scripts print are compared with the paper together with the R output and any self-reproducible output (JASP/jamovi, SPSS-Viewer, notebook outputs).

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How PubPeer Comments works

This module checks references and warns for citations that have comments on pubpeer (excluding Statcheck comments).

The PubPeer module uses the PubPeer API to check for each reference that has a DOI whether there are comments on the post-publication peer review platform. If comments are found, a link to the comments is provided. Comments by ‘Statcheck’ on PubPeer are ignored, see https://retractionwatch.com/2016/09/02/heres-why-more-than-50000-psychology-studies-are-about-to-have-pubpeer-entries/.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

For more information, see PubPeer.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How RetractionWatch works

This module checks references and warns for citations in the RetractionWatch Database.

The RetractionWatch Check module compares the reference list against studies in the RetractionWatch database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that the study was retracted, has an expression of concern, or a correction.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

It is possible the authors are already aware that a study was retracted, but the module can’t evaluate this.

The database can be manually updated with the rw_update function. For more information, see https://gitlab.com/crossref/retraction-watch-data.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Replication Check works

This module checks references and warns for citations of original studies for which replication or reproduction studies exist in the FLoRA database.

The Replication Check module compares the reference list against studies in the FLoRA (FORRT Library of Replication Attempts) database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that a replication or reproduction of the original study exists, and should be cited. No reminder is given when the paper already cites that replication or reproduction (matched by its DOI).

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

It is possible the original study was cited for other reasons than the empirical claim tested, or that the replication/reproduction in the FLoRA database is for only one of the studies in the paper, and not the study the authors discuss.

The database can be manually updated with the FLoRA_update() function. For more information, see https://forrt.org/FLoRA/.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens, Lisa DeBruine and Lukas Wallrich

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Full text

Abstract

Artificial agents as leaders and followers: An integrative role-congruity review. Group & Organization Management.

Artificial Agents as Leaders and Followers: An Integrative Role-Congruity Review

"I like robots. I like them considerably better than I do human beings. If a robot can be created capable of being a civil executive, I think he'd make the best one possible. By the Laws of Robotics, he'd be incapable of harming humans, incapable of tyranny, of corruption, of stupidity, of prejudice. […]" -Dr. Susan Calvin. Isaac Asimov, I, Robot (1950).

This quote from Asimov's science-fiction short story "Evidence" (Asimov, 1950) foreshadows a phenomenon that has become increasingly salient in organizations: the emergence of robots and artificial intelligence systems as managerial actors (e.g., Tsai, Marshall, et al., 2022; Wesche & Sonderegger, 2019). On digital platforms, algorithmic systems already allocate work, monitor performance, and issue feedback (e.g., Curchod et al., 2020; Rahman, 2021).

Recent EU-representative worker surveys show that about one in five workers is already managed by software: between 16% and 27% report that algorithmic systems allocate tasks or monitor performance (Riso et al., 2025). Further, organizations are beginning to experiment with embodied forms such as virtual agents, including digital twins of executives that can attend meetings or respond to employee inquiries on their behalf (Boyle, 2025). However, hopes that algorithmic or robotic managers can deliver objective and bias-free decisions (e.g., Chamorro-Premuzic & Ahmetoglu, 2016) exist alongside perceptions that such nonhuman agents lack relevant social-emotional capacities such as empathy, care, and emotional understanding (e.g., Yam et al., 2025). This reveals a tension between nonhuman agents occupying leader roles and the human-centric nature of leadership (Tsai, Marshall, et al., 2022).

Empirical findings mirror this tension by revealing considerable inconsistencies: Perceptions can shift depending on the ascribed organizational role. For example, an identical chatbot labeled an "AI-based manager" was evaluated as more likable and knowledgeable than when framed as an "AI representative" (Jeon, 2022). Adding to the complexity, perceptions also vary by design: robots high in human likeness can increase perceptions of warmth and acceptance (e.g., Wolf & Stock-Homburg, 2026), yet also be viewed as more abusive (e.g., Yam et al., 2022). Some studies show that algorithmic or robotic leaders are perceived as competent, fair, or even preferable to human leaders (e.g., Höddinghaus et al., 2021; Wolf & Stock-Homburg, 2026), whereas others find that they evoke discomfort, distrust, or illegitimacy (e.g., Jago et al., 2024; McGuire & De Cremer, 2023).

This pattern of mixed reactions to the same artificial agent across roles and designs points to a common evaluative mechanism: the perceived fit, or lack of fit, between what a role is seen to require and what the agent appears to offer. Role congruity theory (Eagly & Karau, 2002) explains this mechanism by comparing attributes that are prescribed by a professional role (e.g., what a leader should be like) and attributes that are ascribed on the basis of social categories such as gender or age (Eagly, 1987; Eagly & Wood, 2012; Koenig & Eagly, 2014). When prescribed role expectations and ascribed attributes are perceived as congruent, targets are seen as appropriate and legitimate; when they are perceived as incongruent, observers are more likely to respond with bias or resistance (del Carmen Triana et al., 2024; Eagly & Karau, 2002).

As algorithmic systems and robots are perceived as social actors in organizations (e.g., Anthony et al., 2023), we extend this logic of role (in)congruity to nonhuman agents. We define these artificial agents as machine-based systems that operate with varying autonomy, adapt after deployment, and generate outputs such as predictions, recommendations, or decisions that influence physical or virtual environments (European Union, 2024). Whereas humans in organizational roles are evaluated through social categories that shape perceived role incongruity, artificial agents lack such category memberships. Instead, we argue that observers rely on design-based social cues, such as embodiment (e.g., algorithm vs. robot) and verbal and non-verbal behavior (e.g., voice, conversational style, name), to infer an agent's attributes (e.g., Blut et al., 2021; Glikson & Woolley, 2020). When the attributes inferred from these cues do not fit those prescribed by the role, observers perceive role incongruity, shaping how they evaluate, respond to, and adapt to artificial agents in organizational roles (del Carmen Triana et al., 2024).

This lack-of-fit mechanism clarifies why the same artificial agent may be evaluated differently across roles and designs.

Research has typically examined roles and design cues separately rather than together.

Work on artificial agents in organizations specified how these agents can occupy leader, partner, and follower roles (Tsai, Marshall, et al., 2022; Wesche & Sonderegger, 2019) and, in particular, when they can function as teammates in human-agent teams (e.g., Berretta et al., 2023; Bezrukova et al., 2023; Lyons et al., 2021; Seeber et al., 2020; Wolf & Stock-Homburg, 2023; Wynne & Lyons, 2018). Design-focused reviews show that appearance and verbal and nonverbal behavioral cues evoke perceptions of human likeness and mind perceptions that increase perceived competence and warmth and shape acceptance (e.g., Blut et al., 2021; Liew & Tan, 2021; Wirtz et al., 2018). Trust has emerged as one focal outcome of these evaluations, and trustfocused reviews highlight performance, transparency, context, and mind perception as central antecedents of trust in artificial teammates (e.g., Benk et al., 2025; Dang & Li, 2026; Glikson & Woolley, 2020; Hancock et al., 2021; Vanneste & Puranam, 2025; Wildman et al., 2024). Yet, roles and design cues have rarely been considered together, with only a few exceptions in preliminary work on machine replacement that adopt a narrower mind-role fit perspective (Dong et al., 2026; Yam et al., 2025). We therefore focus on role incongruity as the underlying mechanism and combine organizational role expectations with design-based social cues to explain when and why people accept or resist artificial agents as leaders, followers, and partners.

In this integrative review, we map and explain inconsistencies in prior research on artificial agents in various organizational roles by uncovering the mechanism through which observers evaluate artificial agents as legitimate or illegitimate occupants of leader, follower, or partner roles. To this end, we systematically review 107 empirical studies across management, psychology, engineering, and computer science, apply role incongruity as the core explanatory lens (Eagly & Karau, 2002), and later integrate the recurring patterns into a conceptual model.

When prescribed role expectations and ascribed attributes are perceived as congruent, artificial agents are judged as legitimate role occupants; when they are perceived as incongruent, illegitimacy perceptions dominate. We further organize the breadth of indicators used in (in)congruity evaluations into two fundamental dimensions of social perception: a task dimension capturing competence and assertiveness and a social-emotional dimension comprising warmth and morality (Abele et al., 2021). We focus on these dimensions because they summarize the criteria by which leader and follower roles are evaluated (e.g., Benson et al., 2024; Nichols et al., 2026).

Following a human-centric perspective on emerging technologies (e.g., Parker et al., 2026), we place observers' perceptions at the center of the analysis: Whether an artificial agent can function in an organizational role depends both on its capabilities and on the people who work with it. With our review, we contribute to this understanding in three ways. First, we organize and integrate a large and fragmented body of evidence spanning management, psychology, engineering, and computer science, and show that a role-incongruity lens extracts coherent patterns from findings that had appeared inconsistent: competent-yet-cold evaluations of agent leaders, relative congruity for agent followers, and conditional acceptance of agent partners and teammates. Second, weaving in the recurring patterns identified in our review, we extend role congruity theory to artificial agents through the Artificial Agent Role Congruity (AARC) model, explaining how these differing evaluations emerge from the fit or misfit between attributes inferred from design-based cues and attributes prescribed for organizational roles. This clarifies that established role mechanisms apply when the role occupant is nonhuman, answering calls to ground human-AI research in established psychological theory (Bigman et al., 2026). Third, we set an AARC-based agenda for future research on role-design fit, spanning theoretical, methodological, and practical questions.

Conceptual Background

In this integrative review, we combine role congruity theory (del Carmen Triana et al., 2024; Eagly & Karau, 2002) with research on human-robot and human-agent collaboration (e.g., Tsai, Marshall, et al., 2022; Yam et al., 2025) into an evaluative lens for how observers perceive artificial agents in organizational roles. We organize our review around three elements: (1) organizational roles as prescriptive expectations (leader, follower, partner), (2) design-based features as social cues from which observers ascribe attributes, and (3) the comparison between prescribed role expectations and ascribed attributes underlying perceived role (in)congruity.

Role Congruity as Core Mechanism

Within organizations, actors occupy roles that carry distinct functions and expectations.

Role theory conceptualizes these expectations as socially shared sets of norms that define appropriate attributes and behavior for occupants of specific positions (Anglin et al., 2022; Biddle, 1986). As a consequence, roles provide a reference for evaluation; observers compare what the organizational role prescribes with how they perceive a target. Role congruity theory (Eagly & Karau, 2002) formalizes this evaluative mechanism as a comparison between two sets of expectations: the attributes prescribed by a functional role, such as a leader or follower role, and the attributes ascribed to a target based on a salient social category (e.g., gender; Eagly & Wood, 2012; Koenig & Eagly, 2014). When these attributes are incongruent, observers perceive or anticipate a lack of fit and respond with bias or resistance (Eagly & Karau, 2002; Heilman, 1983). For example, when leader roles emphasize assertiveness and authority, individuals who are instead stereotyped as more communal and less agentic may be evaluated as less congruent with these roles (Koenig et al., 2011).

We organize perceived (in)congruity around two fundamental dimensions of social perception: a task dimension capturing competence and assertiveness, and a social-emotional dimension capturing warmth and morality (Abele et al., 2016 (Abele et al., , 2021)). Competence and assertiveness both concern accomplishing work and exerting influence, whereas warmth and morality both concern relating to others, and they relate more strongly within than between the two dimensions (Koch et al., 2024; Yzerbyt et al., 2025). Observers perceive a target along these dimensions and compare this perception with what the role prescribes; (in)congruity is the fit or misfit between the two.

Further, management research distinguishes three classes of role-incongruity effects: target evaluations, target outcomes, and target adaptation (del Carmen Triana et al., 2024). We use these evaluations, such as perceived effectiveness and favorability, as correlates of the task and social-emotional dimensions (see Table 1). The other two are consequences: outcomes are the behavioral and material results, such as selection, promotion, compensation, or dismissal, and adaptation refers to the adjustments a target makes in response to role incongruity, such as selfregulation or role reframing. Abele et al. (2016 Abele et al. ( , 2021)). The task and socialemotional labels follow the classic distinction in research on groups and leadership (Bales, 1950); Abele et al. (2021) refer to these higher-order dimensions as vertical and horizontal. We retain the subdimensions and report them separately; the two dimensions organize rather than collapse them. The final column lists illustrative correlates of each subdimension (Abele & Wojciszke, 2014).

Role (In)congruity of Artificial Agents

Role congruity theory has been expanded beyond its original focus on gender and leadership to encompass a broader range of social roles and characteristics (for a review, see del Carmen Triana et al., 2024). For instance, role incongruity can emerge when organizational role expectations conflict with attributes associated with race (e.g., Paluch & Shum, 2024; Sy et al., 2010), age (Daldrop et al., 2025; Diekman & Hirnisey, 2007), or sexual orientation (Liberman & Golom, 2015; Rule et al., 2016).

We extend this theory to explain how people judge artificial agents in organizational roles.

While role congruity theory traditionally focuses on human social categories, artificial agents have become salient social actors in organizations (Anthony et al., 2023; Harris-Watson & Larson, 2026). This continues the "computers are social actors" (CASA) paradigm, which showed that people apply human social rules and expectations to computers and other artificial agents (Nass et al., 1994 (Nass et al., , 1996)). People hold mental models about robotic and algorithmic agents (Banks, 2020; Cena & Grasso, 2026) and apply social perceptions to them (Nass & Moon, 2000).

Because they evaluate these agents along the same dimensions that guide human judgments, such as competence and warmth (e.g., Harris-Watson et al., 2023; McKee et al., 2024), artificial agents can be perceived as congruent or incongruent with their organizational roles, much as human role occupants are.

Artificial agents increasingly occupy a range of organizational roles, including follower, partner, and even leader roles (e.g., Tsai, Marshall, et al., 2022; Wesche & Sonderegger, 2019).

Whether an agent is perceived as congruent or incongruent therefore depends on what its role prescribes, which we turn to next.

Organizational Roles: Leader, Follower, and Partner

Organizational roles function as shared cognitive schemas that define expectations for behavior and abilities within hierarchical and collaborative structures (Anglin et al., 2022).

Research on implicit leadership and followership theories demonstrates that people hold mental models of leaders and followers: sets of attributes and behaviors that describe ideal members of each category (Lord et al., 1984 (Lord et al., , 2020)).

Leaders are expected to provide direction, coordination, and influence and to score high on both the task and the social-emotional dimension (Junker & van Dick, 2014). On the task dimension, leaders should be competent, able to analyze, decide, and execute effectively, and appropriately assertive; assertiveness is seen as ideal when it guides and coordinates action but counter-ideal when expressed as dominance (Benson et al., 2024; Offermann & Coats, 2018). On the social-emotional dimension, leaders are expected to show moral integrity, that is, fairness, honesty, and responsibility for others' welfare, alongside warmth (Den Hartog et al., 1999; Offermann & Coats, 2018). These social-emotional qualities are central to ideal-leader expectations and can even take precedence over assertiveness in followers' preferences (Daldrop et al., 2025; Ponce de Leon & Bailey, 2025).

Followers are expected to be dependable, conscientious, and loyal, carrying out directives and supporting collective goals (Junker et al., 2016; Sy, 2010). On the task dimension, expectations are modest: followers need enough competence to perform reliably, while assertiveness is expected to stay low, since dominance or overt self-promotion contradicts the deferential nature of followership (Bastardoz & Van Vugt, 2019; Benson et al., 2024). On the social-emotional dimension, the central expectations are warmth and supportive engagement, centered on cooperation rather than the moral authority expected of leaders (Benson et al., 2024; Junker et al., 2016).

People also hold expectations of partners, including teammates and colleagues, who collaborate as equals rather than within a hierarchy of authority. On the task dimension, expectations are moderate: partners should be competent enough to contribute reliably and to coordinate and communicate with others, but without the directive authority expected of leaders (Stevens & Campion, 1994). On the social-emotional dimension, warmth carries particular weight among equals rather than the deference expected of followers; people favor warm, cooperative colleagues and, when choosing whom to work with, interpersonal liking can even outweigh competence (Casciaro & Lobo, 2008).

We have outlined what observers expect of leaders, followers, and partners. But how do people assess the fit between an artificial agent and the expectations of these roles? We argue that observers rely on the artificial agent's design-based social cues to ascribe attributes that make it appear congruent or incongruent with leader, follower, or partner roles.

From Design-Based Cues to Ascribed Attributes

Design-based social cues are the appearance and behavioral features designed into an artificial agent that signal its social presence to observers (Blut et al., 2021; Liew & Tan, 2021).

These cues fall into two broad categories: appearance and verbal and non-verbal behavior.

Appearance cues capture embodiment, an agent's form, degree of human likeness, and movement quality, ranging from disembodied algorithmic systems and voice-based assistants to virtual avatars and physically embodied robots (Glikson & Woolley, 2020; Liew & Tan, 2021).

Verbal and non-verbal behavioral cues include communicative features such as voice, language style, responsiveness and turn-taking, gaze, gesture, facial expression, and labeling (Blut et al., 2021; Liew & Tan, 2021). Such communicative cues, for example polite or empathic phrasing, increase perceived social presence and human likeness (Banks et al., 2023; Jeon, 2022).

Design-based social cues, particularly those conveying social presence and human likeness, may enable people to infer specific attributes of the agent (Epley et al., 2007; Liew & Tan, 2021). One potential route from social cues to ascribed attributes runs through mind perception: observers infer the extent of the agent's mind along two capacities, agency (i.e., the capacity for self-control and planning) and experience (i.e., the capacity to feel and sense; Gray et al., 2007; Waytz et al., 2010). These two capacities map loosely onto the same task and socialemotional dimensions that structure role expectations (e.g., Au & Ng, 2021). On the task dimension, perceived agency corresponds most clearly with competence and assertiveness. On the social-emotional dimension of role attributions, perceived experience corresponds with warmth, whereas both perceived agency and perceived experience map onto morality, the second facet of the social-emotional dimension (Gray et al., 2012). For example, social cues that signal feeling, such as a humanlike voice or empathic language, raise perceived experience as part of an artificial agent's mind perception and, in turn, its warmth and benevolence attributions (Koban & Banks, 2024; Yam et al., 2021). Table 2 summarizes how specific design features map onto these mind perceptions and ascribed attributes.

Recent research increasingly uses mind perception to explain how people relate to artificial agents at work: as a framework for human-agent teams (Harris-Watson & Larson, 2026), as a driver of how employees craft their relationships with industrial robots (Goštautaitė et al., 2026), and as an account of threat and fear of replacement across professional roles, such as doctors and judges, and personal ones, such as companions (Dong et al., 2026; Yam et al., 2025). Part of this work already draws on a lack-of-fit logic: whether people accept or resist a machine in a given role depends on how well the mind they ascribe to it matches the mind the role is seen to require (Dong et al., 2026; Yam et al., 2025).

Our review applies a role-congruity lens beyond mind perception, to role ( in summarize meta-analytic and review findings on embodiment and communicative behavior (Blut et al., 2021; Liew & Tan, 2021), linked to mind-perception theory (Gray et al., 2007; Waytz et al., 2010) and the task/social-emotional evaluation dimensions (Abele et al., 2016 (Abele et al., , 2021)). This synthesis informed the coding scheme of our review (see Method).

Method

To answer our research question, we conducted a systematic review of the empirical literature on how people evaluate artificial agents in organizational roles. The Online Supplement, coding materials, and complete coded dataset are available on the Open Science Framework (https://osf.io/582jn/ overview).

Literature Search

We searched PsycINFO, Business Source Premier, and IEEE Xplore in June 2025, combining artificial-agent terms with social-perception constructs, and supplemented the database results with backward and forward citation tracing and targeted journal searches (full search string and per-source counts in Section A of the Online Supplement).

Inclusion and Exclusion Criteria

We included peer-reviewed empirical studies with human participants that examined an artificial agent (i.e., robot, AI system, avatar, or algorithm) in an organizational or task context and reported at least one perception of, or evaluative or acceptance response to, the agent in a leader, follower, or partner role. We restricted the review to studies that frame artificial agents as occupants of organizational roles, where role prescriptions are at stake. We excluded purely technical or conceptual papers, consumer-only studies without a work or team setting, and studies whose measured outcomes concerned only the human worker, third parties, or a nonagent object (full criteria in Section A of the Online Supplement).

Screening and Selection

Screening followed PRISMA guidelines (Page et al., 2021): after deduplication, records were screened at title, abstract, and full text against these criteria, manually, with uncertain cases resolved by the author team and no automated tools used to exclude records. Studies excluded at full-text screening are summarized in Section E of the Online Supplement. Figure 1 shows the resulting flow. PRISMA Flow Diagram of the Identification and Screening Process Note. Figure 1 is presented in accordance with PRISMA guidelines and details the flow of records through identification, screening, eligibility, and final inclusion (Page et al., 2021).

Coding Procedure and Scheme Development

Consistent with the iterative nature of integrative reviews (Cronin & George, 2023), a preliminary coding scheme developed by two trained research assistants on overlapping subsets was revised and applied to all included studies by the lead author. For each study we coded the agent's assigned role, that is, leader, follower, partner, or team member (Tsai, Marshall, et al., 2022); its design-based cues, both appearance and verbal and non-verbal behavior (Blut et al., 2021; Glikson & Woolley, 2020); the evaluation on the task (competence, assertiveness) and social-emotional (warmth, morality) dimensions (Abele et al., 2021); responses to role (in)congruity; and contextual variables (domain, research design, and sample region). The full coding documentation, including the codebook and scheme development, is provided in the Online Supplement (Sections B and C), and descriptive characteristics of the included studies are reported in Section D.

Results

We reviewed 107 empirical studies to identify evaluations indicating role congruity or role incongruity. Guided by role congruity theory (Eagly & Karau, 2002), we identified role incongruity when evaluations are inconsistent with the attributes a role is expected to require.

We classified evaluations as incongruent when studies report lower evaluations on role-central facets (e.g., lower competence or morality for leaders; lower reliability-linked competence for followers; lower warmth or morality for partners) or evaluations that signal a violation of role expectations (e.g., excessive assertiveness for followers). In contrast, we classified evaluations as congruent when they indicate alignment with role-central facets and do not show evidence of role violations.

Using this rule set, we examine how an agent's assigned role and its design-based social cues relate to evaluations, and we summarize recurring patterns of congruity and incongruity by role. Summary tables present representative studies for agents as leaders (Table A1), followers (Table A2), and partners or teammates (Table A3); the full coded dataset is available on the Open Science Framework (https://osf.io/582jn/).

Overview and Descriptive Analysis

The review reveals a recent acceleration in research on perceptions of artificial agents in organizational or task-related roles, with 89% of studies published in 2020 or later (95 of 107).

Work prior to 2015 primarily consisted of social robotics and trust experiments (e.g., Salem et al., 2013), while the period from 2016 to 2019 marked the first integration of workplace-relevant contexts linking human likeness to evaluations of competence and warmth (Castro-González et al., 2016; Lee, 2018). Research starting in 2020 increasingly addressed algorithmic and robotic leadership (e.g., Jago et al., 2024; McGuire & De Cremer, 2023), teamwork and collaboration (e.g., Fraune et al., 2020; Harris-Watson et al., 2023), and service or caregiving settings (e.g., Paluch et al., 2022; Yam et al., 2021). Across disciplines, leader-role research is concentrated in management and psychology, partner-and teammate-role research in robotics, engineering, and computer science, and follower-role research is spread more evenly across fields. Detailed distributions by publication year, disciplinary field, primary context, agent design type, research design, and sample region are provided in the Online Supplement Section D (Tables S5-S11).

Role Incongruity of Artificial Agents as Leaders

A recurring pattern emerges for artificial agents in leader roles: they are often credited with task competence but fall short on the warmth, benevolence, and legitimacy that leadership also requires. On the task dimension, artificial leaders are frequently judged competent. An AI avatar delivering a vision was rated more competent, though less warm, than a human delivering the same vision (Buss et al., 2025); robotic managers are accepted more readily on performancerelated than on relational aspects (Wolf & Stock-Homburg, 2026). Automated leaders can also be seen as comparable in ability and higher in consistency and transparency than human leaders (Höddinghaus et al., 2021).

The consistent deficit is on the social-emotional dimension. Artificial leaders and their decisions are perceived as less fair, trustworthy, and benevolent than human counterparts (Li & Bitterly, 2024; McGuire & De Cremer, 2023; Wesche et al., 2024), particularly for decisions seen to require human judgment (Lee, 2018). Anthropomorphic and agentic design cues do not reliably close this gap and can even widen it: humanlike robot supervisors elicit greater perceived agency and, in turn, perceptions of abuse and supervisor-directed retaliation when delivering negative feedback (Yam et al., 2022), and employees comply less with unethical instructions from AI than from human supervisors (Lanz et al., 2024).

Overall, the evidence points to a competent-but-cold pattern: artificial leaders can meet competence expectations yet fall short on the benevolence, fairness, and legitimacy that confer standing in leader roles, an incongruity concentrated on the social-emotional dimension. We present sample studies for agents as leaders in Table A1.

Role Congruity of Artificial Agents as Followers

For artificial agents in follower or subordinate roles (e.g., service, assistive, or support functions), the evidence points to congruity between design-based cues and follower-role expectations. When design features signal social responsiveness without asserting authority, agents are generally evaluated as both warm and competent (Yang et al., 2025).

Anthropomorphic cues that convey emotional capacity raise perceived experience and satisfaction (Yam et al., 2021) and foster relational investment, including parasocial bonds with the agent (Noor et al., 2022), while warm, attentive service agents enhance psychological ownership and revisit intentions (Ruiz-Equihua et al., 2023). Functional cues that signal reliability and capability, in turn, build the competence-based trust that drives adoption (Kraus et al., 2024).

The pattern reverses mainly when human-likeness becomes too pronounced: a highly anthropomorphic, physically present driving agent lowered perceived control and cognitive trust relative to a virtual one (Cheng et al., 2022). On balance, the follower evidence supports role congruity, with most design-based cues aligning with follower-role expectations for warmth, responsiveness, and dependable performance. This evidence base is concentrated in service and caregiving contexts (23 of 37 follower studies, 62%; 18 retail or service and 5 healthcare) and includes only two internal-office studies, so we see the congruity pattern as most directly applicable to service and caregiving settings. We present sample studies for agents as followers in Table A2.

Role (In)congruity of Artificial Agents as Partners and Teammates

For artificial agents positioned as partners or team members, evaluations are mixed and depend on boundary conditions. Agents that signal prosocial orientation and competence are generally accepted: prosocial (high social-value-orientation) agents are rated warmer, and warmth drives partner preference and continued cooperation beyond objective performance (McKee et al., 2024). A more humanlike agent framed as a teammate also elicits greater initial trust and willingness to collaborate (Mirowska & Arsenyan, 2025).

Acceptance erodes when an agent falls short of the expectations its framing creates. A "colleague" framing raises expectations that an error-prone agent fails to meet, creating frustration (Einola et al., 2024); moral violations reduce trust more sharply than equally consequential performance failures (Rezaei Khavas et al., 2024); and strongly anthropomorphic collaborative robots raise perceived intelligence and, in turn, perceived threat and negative attitudes (Liao et al., 2024). Team composition matters as well: interpersonal trust and identification are lower in two-member human-AI dyads than in all-human dyads, though this pattern was attenuated in three-member teams (Georganta & Ulfert, 2024).

Collectively, these findings tie evaluations of partner and teammate agents to the interaction between role prescriptions for reciprocity and cooperation and the attributes inferred from design cues. Role congruity emerges when cues signal enough social presence and experience to support trust and coordination without so much agency or autonomy that they violate relational expectations; role incongruity emerges when anthropomorphic or agentic cues exceed those expectations, eliciting discomfort or threat. We present sample studies for agents as partners or team members in Table A3.

Toward the Artificial Agent Role Congruity (AARC) Model

We develop the Artificial Agent Role Congruity (AARC) model by extending role congruity theory (Eagly & Karau, 2002) to artificial agents. AARC formalizes how observers compare prescribed expectations of organizational roles with ascribed attributes inferred from an artificial agent's design (Figure 2), producing perceptions of role congruity or role incongruity.

We present AARC as a conceptual model (Rousseau, 2026) that invites empirical testing and refinement. The model organizes the evidence reviewed above and identifies when, how, and why artificial agents are evaluated differently across organizational roles. Note. The AARC model illustrates how role-based expectations and design-based social cues shape evaluations of artificial agents. Two sets of expectations, the prescriptions of the organizational role and the artificial-agent schema, are compared with the attributes ascribed to the agent. Design-based social cues are read, through mind perception (agency and experience), as ascribed attributes along two dimensions of social perception. Perceived role (in)congruity is the fit or misfit between these ascribed attributes and what the role and schema prescribe, which in turn shapes evaluative outcomes and adaptations such as acceptance, trust, and legitimacy. As our review through a role incongruity lens suggests, the combination of roles and design features determines how artificial agents are evaluated. Whereas role congruity is attained when role and design match, two types of role incongruity are conceivable. In roles > design configurations, the expectations attached to functional or organizational roles exceed what the agent's design appears to offer. Agents framed as leaders, colleagues, or partners are expected to display empathy and moral intent, yet their design typically signals limited social presence or experience.

This shortcoming may generate a deficit on the social-emotional dimension, leading observers to perceive them as tools or morally inadequate actors (Einola & Khoreva, 2023; McGuire & De Cremer, 2023). In contrast, design > roles configurations occur when an agent's design implies more agency, dominance, or human likeness than its role requires. For example, anthropomorphic or socially assertive cobots may appear overly competent or intrusive in subordinate or tool roles, causing threat or unease (Liao et al., 2023 (Liao et al., , 2024;; Stanton & Stevens, 2017). Both patterns reduce congruity between role-based prescriptions and design-based social cues, thereby defining the core antecedent of role incongruity in line with the AARC model.

Taken together, the AARC model adds three elements beyond role congruity theory applied to a new class of role occupants. First, in artificial agent evaluation design-based social cues replace social-category cues as the source of ascribed attributes. What gender, age, or social class signal for role congruity evaluations in research on human role occupants, embodiment, voice, language, and labeling signal for artificial agents; because designers can control these cues, ascribed attributes become a design parameter rather than a demographic given. Second, mind perception bridges cues and evaluation: perceived agency feeds the task dimension and perceived experience the social-emotional dimension, so the model predicts that experience cues carry more evaluative weight in roles whose prescriptions emphasize warmth and morality.

Third, the artificial agent schema-that is, the shared expectations about how robots or algorithms should act and relate (e.g., reliability, precision, obedience, neutrality, cooperation;

Banks, 2020)-constitutes a second layer of (in)congruity, so an agent can satisfy its organizational role prescriptions yet violate this artificial agent schema. Together, these elements specify the extent of perceived misfit and why it emerges, rather than merely noting that artificial agents differ from human role occupants.

Discussion

With this review, we asked how role-based expectations and design-based inferences jointly shape evaluations of artificial agents in organizational contexts. Three patterns emerged.

First, artificial agents in leader roles often face role incongruity, particularly on the socialemotional dimension. Second, agents in follower roles generally elicit congruity, especially in service and caregiving settings. Third, congruity in partner roles depends on design and context.

These patterns represent the AARC model's mechanism: role prescriptions and the attributes inferred from design jointly shape evaluations on the task (competence, assertiveness) and social-emotional (warmth, morality) dimensions (Abele et al., 2021). Across roles, incongruity took two forms: either role demands exceeded what the design conveyed (roles > design), or the design implied more agency or human likeness than the role required (design > roles). Notably, humanlike design rarely repairs the leader-role deficit; if anything, greater human likeness intensifies discomfort by amplifying perceived agency without a matching gain in perceived moral understanding, consistent with accounts of AI trust as resting jointly on morality and performance (Everett et al., 2026).

Theoretical Implications

Our review suggests three theoretical extensions. The first links the model to the mindperception account of machine replacement. That literature frames reactions to machines as a matter of mind-role fit, the correspondence between the mind ascribed to an agent and the mind a role is taken to require (Dong et al., 2026; Yam et al., 2025). In role-congruity terms, this is a lack of fit between prescribed and ascribed attributes in which the ascribed attributes are the agent's perceived mind; mind-role (mis)fit is therefore a special case of role (in)congruity rather than a separate mechanism. Because perceived mind comprises two capacities, agency and experience, the dimension on which the lack of fit appears is predictable. In leader roles, incongruity arises when design cues convey agency but little experience, so that agents are judged competent yet lacking in warmth and moral intent (e.g., McGuire & De Cremer, 2023; Rubin et al., 2025). In follower roles, the lack of fit reverses, as mind-like cues exceed what a controllable, impersonal role prescribes (e.g., Appel et al., 2020). In partner and teammate roles, a comparable surplus of agency or deficit of experience conflicts with the reciprocity and warmth these roles prescribe (e.g., Banks et al., 2023). Framed this way, the mind-perception account of machine replacement is subsumed within the broader role-congruity logic of the AARC model.

Within our AARC model, an agent's design not only constitutes a set of cues but can also serve as a baseline "artificial agent role" schema. When an agent's behavior or perceived attributes violate this artificial agent schema, observers may perceive a role violation that is comparable to violating a social role such as the gender role in role congruity theory, where prejudice can arise from failing to meet the prescriptions of a social role over and above the functional role itself (Eagly & Karau, 2002). Consequently, negative reactions may stem not only from failing to meet leader-, partner-, or follower-role prescriptions but also from not fulfilling the norms of being a "good robot" or "trustworthy algorithm." Empirical evidence illustrates this dynamic: agents that make mistakes (Dietvorst et al., 2015; Weidemann & Russwinkel, 2021), act unpredictably (Salem et al., 2013), cheat (Herath et al., 2018), or display humanlike affect or behavior such as anger (Herdel et al., 2024), dialect (Kühne et al., 2023), or humming (Liu et al., 2023) violate this artificial agent schema. Such violations can lead observers to downgrade agents to malfunctioning tools or react with abjection (Banks et al., 2023; Einola & Khoreva, 2023), while in other cases they "upgrade" agents to social actors due to an increase in mind perception (as this behavior is more humanlike). Integrating this artificial agent schema into the AARC model adds a second dimension of role incongruity in artificial agent evaluation, arising not only from mismatched organizational roles but also from deviations from the normative expectations embedded in the artificial agent role itself.

The reviewed evidence also points to a contingency that the model does not yet formalize: the context in which a role is embedded shapes how strongly its prescriptions emphasize the task versus the social-emotional dimension.

The composition of our database illustrates this. For agents in follower roles, evidence is concentrated in service and caregiving settings (23 of 37 studies, 62%), where role prescriptions emphasize warmth and responsiveness; the congruity we observe is therefore most directly a statement about service contexts. Leader-role evidence comes predominantly from internal employee and management settings (10 of 17 studies, 59%), where moral and relational prescriptions are most demanding, which sharpens the incongruity we observe. We propose that artificial agents will be evaluated as less incongruent in leader roles whose context emphasizes task prescriptions over social-emotional ones, such as data-intensive allocation or monitoring tasks, and as more incongruent in follower or partner roles whose context elevates social-emotional prescriptions, such as care work. We therefore treat the generalizability of our role-specific conclusions accordingly (see the scope and boundary conditions in the Online Supplement, Section A).

Practical Implications

Our findings translate into concrete guidance for managers deploying artificial agents and for designers building them (Chhillar & Aguilera, 2022; Kellogg et al., 2020). For managers, three priorities stand out. First, retain human moral oversight in algorithmic management: hybrid human-in-the-loop arrangements increase trust and perceived fairness compared to full automation (De Cremer & McGuire, 2022; Kares et al., 2023). Second, configure leadership as augmentation rather than replacement: combining algorithmic capabilities with human judgment addresses the moral and relational deficits that design alone cannot repair (Raisch & Fomina, 2025; Raisch & Krakowski, 2021). Field evidence shows that the governance of such arrangements can be configured so that organizations and workers benefit jointly (Kellogg, 2022). Third, specify the role frame explicitly when introducing an agent: role ambiguity produces unmet "colleague" expectations and disappointment, whereas clear framing aligns observer expectations with what the agent can deliver (Einola et al., 2024).

For designers, three principles follow. First, do not assume that anthropomorphizing agents intended for leader roles will resolve perceived moral deficits: humanlike cues may amplify rather than reduce role incongruity (McGuire & De Cremer, 2023; Yam et al., 2022). Second, design for social presence rather than human likeness: responsiveness, relational language, and transparent identity framing build acceptance without uncanny-valley risks. Third, match design cues to the role: human likeness is well received in follower roles, accepted conditionally in partner roles, and rarely fitting in leader roles.

These principles rest on convergent evidence: social presence matters more for acceptance and cooperation than humanlike appearance (Elshan et al., 2022). Presence cues such as relational language, immediacy, and transparent identity framing raise trust and willingness to rely on the system. Humanlike appearance alone, however, may be insufficient to support social presence in human-AI collaboration (Siemon et al., 2026). Strong cues can also backfire: excessive perceived autonomy reduces cooperation through a reactance process of "agency-cued abjection," a feeling of unease and rejection (Banks et al., 2023), and reading an agent's outputs as signs of "reasoning" encourages false confidence in its capability (Kambhampati et al., 2026).

More broadly, these prescriptions show what human-centric deployment of artificial agents requires in practice: respecting the role expectations through which workers grant legitimacy to a technology, so that efficiency gains do not come at the cost of the warmth, morality, and fairness that make any role occupant acceptable.

Limitations

Our review has limitations. First, the database covers peer-reviewed journal articles, so cutting-edge findings from conference proceedings in cognate fields such as computer science and human-robot interaction may be missing. Future reviews could broaden the inclusion criteria to high-quality conference proceedings to mitigate this. Second, the final coding scheme was applied by the lead author after a two-phase scheme development. Although the procedure is described in the Method, the inter-coder checks are documented in the Online Supplement, and the full coding is openly available, single-coder application remains a constraint. Third, the evidence base is unevenly distributed across contexts: follower-role evidence concentrates in service and caregiving settings and leader-role evidence in internal organizational settings. This calls for further investigations into the generalizability of role-specific conclusions (see the scope and boundary conditions in the Online Supplement, Section A).

Opportunities for Future Research

Building on the AARC model and our review findings, we outline opportunities in three areas: theory development, measurement and design, and organizational consequences. Table 3 provides an overview of these opportunities, which we elaborate in the following subsections.

Theory Development Role Congruity Versus Role Ambiguity or Conflict.

Role theory distinguishes a structural-functional view, in which roles are relatively fixed positions with prescriptive expectations, and a symbolic-interactionist view, in which roles are negotiated in interactions (Anglin et al., 2022). In the reviewed research, evaluations of artificial agents in leader roles typically follow the structural-functional logic of role congruity theory (del Carmen Triana et al., 2024; Eagly & Karau, 2002): Agents are formally introduced as supervisors or managers, and observers compare prescribed leader expectations with attributes ascribed from design-based cues to judge role congruity or role incongruity. In contrast, studies of artificial agents in partner and follower roles often report patterns that we interpret as role ambiguity or role conflict. We derive this from evidence that participants waver between different role labels and expectations, or explicitly question what the agent "really is," for example when an algorithm formally introduced as a "colleague" was later described by employees as a "shiny tool" when partner role expectations were not met (Einola et al., 2024). Likewise, platform algorithms that fulfill toollike functions (e.g., navigation) and supervisory functions (e.g., giving directives and enforcing performance ratings) blur tool, follower, and leader categories and evoke competing role expectations (Tarafdar et al., 2023). We encourage future research to clarify when evaluations are primarily driven by structural-functional role prescriptions and when they are shaped by negotiated role meanings, and to specify boundary conditions under which role incongruity, role ambiguity, or role conflict best captures responses to artificial agents. Such clarification matters because role prescriptions themselves may shift: some argue that artificial agents could come to address followers' basic psychological needs better than human leaders (Van Quaquebeke & Gerpott, 2023), which would make the leader-role deficits we document contingent rather than constant.

Reversing Human-Machine Hierarchies.

While role (in)congruity represents the primary evaluative mechanism in the AARC model, alternative pathways may also explain resistance toward artificial agents. One such pathway is status incongruity, which occurs when an agent's assigned hierarchical position conflicts with established social norms and expectations (Rudman et al., 2012). Leader roles imply higher status and influence, whereas artificial agents are typically viewed as technologies that support or accompany human workers (Lee & See, 2004).

When this hierarchy is reversed, such as when an artificial agent leads or evaluates human employees, observers may perceive the hierarchy as inappropriate and question the agent's legitimacy (e.g., Jeon, 2022). Similarly, algorithmic management can reduce perceptions of job complexity and status of human organizational members, reinforcing this incongruity between human subordinates and machine supervisors (e.g., Jago et al., 2024). Future research could examine how mind-role and status incongruities jointly influence acceptance across hierarchical contexts.

Measurement and Design

Direct Tests of Role (In)congruity.

There are still almost no direct tests of role (in)congruity or systematic baselines for specific agent designs, with only a few notable exceptions. Some recent studies begin to operationalize role (in)congruity or mind-role (mis)fit by measuring both role requirements and perceived agent characteristics, showing that mismatches between the two can shape perceptions and reactions to artificial agents (Dong et al., 2026). Further, evidence establishing causality for role incongruity as the underlying mechanism remains limited; one exception is experimental work that manipulates mind perception, agency and experience, to test their direct impact on behavior and moral judgment (Lanz et al., 2024).

These early efforts are promising but underscore the need for more empirically rigorous evidence linking mind-role fit to target evaluations and outcomes. In addition, methods such as polynomial regression and response-surface analysis (Tsai, Kim, et al., 2022) could provide a formal approach to testing AARC's proposed mechanisms by explicitly measuring how both role prescriptions and design cues jointly predict perceived fit, enabling direct tests of the "roles > design" and "design > roles" patterns identified in this review.

Research Design Effects. Findings on artificial agents may vary with research design.

Organizational case studies reveal mixed reactions, with negative evaluations of artificial agents coexisting with optimism about their future capabilities or positive implications for employees' work meaningfulness (e.g., Einola et al., 2024; Goštautaitė et al., 2024).

Scenario-based vignette studies may yield more negative responses, potentially because participants imagine rather than experience the interaction (e.g., Liao et al., 2024; Savela et al., 2021), whereas field experiments that combine real task engagement with experimental control suggest that human-agent differences may diminish under real behavioral conditions (Dong et al., 2024).

Research design may therefore function as a boundary condition: direct and sustained interaction may allow initial evaluations to become more differentiated as people gain experience with an artificial agent. Future work should move beyond short vignettes, "Wizard of Oz" experiments (where a hidden human controls the system), and other brief simulations toward designs built on real rather than hypothetical interaction. Even simple, well-controlled lab studies with genuine agent interaction would help, and longitudinal and field designs could track how trust, legitimacy, and cooperation develop over time in real workplaces. Baseline Comparisons as Benchmark. Most research on artificial agents relies on relative comparisons such as human versus agent leaders or high versus low anthropomorphism, without including a baseline in which participants perform the task without guidance from either a human or an artificial agent. Although informative, such designs make it hard to see whether differences reflect genuine role-design effects or artifacts of the comparison. Evidence from our review underscores this concern. In experiments that included a self-directed or "passive" baseline, both human and robot guidance reduced perceived control relative to freely chosen actions, indicating that the loss of agency stemmed from being instructed rather than from the agent's identity (Barlas, 2019).

These findings align with recent methodological critiques that comparisons between two active conditions can produce pseudo effects that may obscure underlying mechanisms (e.g., Stark et al., 2026). Future research should therefore additionally incorporate no-treatment baselines, where appropriate, alongside comparisons with a human leader performing the same task.

Organizational Consequences Team Composition, In-Group Versus Out-Group Categorization, and Role Congruity.

Recent reviews of human-agent teams show that mixed teams frequently cause in-group versus out-group categorization processes, with artificial agents often treated as out-group members (Bezrukova et al., 2023; Wolf & Stock-Homburg, 2023). Examples from our review mirror this pattern: introducing robot teammates reduces identification and psychological attachment, particularly as the number of robot teammates increases (Savela et al., 2021), whereas collaboration improves when at least one additional human partner is present (e.g., Georganta & Ulfert, 2024; Musick et al., 2021). These out-group effects are conceptually distinct from, but consequential for, role (in)congruity of artificial agents as teammates. We therefore propose treating in-group versus out-group categorization as a boundary condition in our AARC model: out-group assignment may lower warmth ascriptions and moral expectations, or shift thresholds for judging fit, potentially overshadowing or amplifying role (in)congruity evaluations. Future research should jointly assess categorization and role-fit processes to clarify when responses to artificial teammates reflect out-group bias, role incongruity, or their combined influence.

Recognition-Based Versus Inference-Based Processes.

Role incongruity of artificial agents in leader roles stems from recognition-based processes (e.g., Lord et al., 2020), where observers rely on design-based cues about who appears to "fit" a leader role. Yet, potential perceptual biases raise a critical question: can demonstrated performance override these (possibly biased) perceptions? Early evidence suggests that algorithmic and AI-assisted managers can, in some contexts, perform effectively enough to challenge the biased judgments.

For example, field studies show that algorithmic supervision can improve service quality and customer satisfaction, even if such gains come with trade-offs in efficiency (Gu et al., 2025).

Large-scale experiments on human-AI collaboration similarly indicate that mixed human-AI teams achieve higher productivity and, in some domains, produce superior output quality relative to human-only teams (Ju & Aral, 2025). These patterns imply that evidence about an agent's actual performance can shift social perception from a recognition-based process, which relies on whether the agent "looks like" a leader, to an inference-based process that places more weight on what the agent demonstrably achieves (Lord et al., 2020).

Spillover to Third Parties.

Artificial agents may reshape how third parties perceive the humans who work with or under them. Early evidence suggests that being managed by an algorithm can change how employees are evaluated by others (Schweitzer & De Cremer, 2024).

Recent evidence extends this pattern along the model's two dimensions: algorithmic management was associated with lower coworkers' affective (warm) trust in newcomers and higher cognitive (task-related) trust (Lin et al., 2026). Such spillover effects on coworkers, applicants, and external observers were rarely measured in the reviewed studies and represent an organizational-level consequence of role (in)congruity that merits systematic attention.

Automation Versus Augmentation.

Our findings suggest that role incongruity for artificial agents acting as leaders is unlikely to be eliminated; it may instead reflect a deeper tension between automation and augmentation (Raisch & Krakowski, 2021). The more decision-making is automated, the greater the need for human oversight and interpretive leadership, so attempts to replace human leaders may increase the demand for distinctly human capabilities such as contextual judgment and social sensemaking. As we note in the practical implications, the remedy lies less in resolving incongruity through design or framing than in reconfiguring leadership into hybrid forms that combine algorithmic input with human judgment (Raisch & Fomina, 2025). Future research should examine how such hybrid architectures can balance analytical augmentation with human interpretive oversight to manage, rather than eliminate, the persistent role incongruity for nonhuman leaders.

Conclusion

Across organizational roles, artificial agents are evaluated very differently: those in leader roles face persistent incongruity, judged competent but short on the morality and legitimacy that leadership demands; those in follower roles show consistent congruity when their design signals responsiveness and reliability; and those in partner or teammate roles fall in between, with congruity conditional on context and design. Our Artificial Agent Role Congruity (AARC) model accounts for this variation: evaluations turn on the (in)congruity between role-based prescriptions and the attributes inferred from design-based cues along the task and socialemotional dimensions of social perception. By extending role congruity theory to nonhuman actors, AARC clarifies when artificial agents are judged as legitimate role occupants and where misfit biases evaluations and outcomes.
